# ParallelScript language specification

ParallelScript is TypeScript compiled ahead of time to native binaries, with a garbage collector and real parallelism. This document is the language reference. It starts from the owner's brief (`docs/BRIEF.md` §2) and grows as features are implemented. **If a behaviour isn't in this spec, it isn't decided.**

Order of authority: `docs/BRIEF.md` (with the owner overrides in `CLAUDE.md`) → this spec → `docs/DECISIONS.md`. Decision ids such as `D-L03` or `D-O05` point into `docs/DECISIONS.md`.

**Status.** Sections 1–8 describe the language as built through M7: milestones M0–M7 (tags `m0` … `m7`), which complete the historical usable-prototype plan. The active post-M7 phase is [HARDENING_PLAN.md](agent/HARDENING_PLAN.md); its [consolidated review ledger](reviews/2026-10-13-final/CONSOLIDATED.md) distinguishes unverified reports, confirmed findings and intentional differences. H01a is a baseline/documentation checkpoint, not a compiler/runtime fix; H01b's independent defect reproductions remain blocked and incomplete. M7 added `pts lsp`, the language server (D-168), the VS Code extension (D-169), hints on tsc's errors, `pts help` and `pts version` (D-170), `pts test` with `pts:test` and `pts:assert` (D-171), and the fixes for the papercuts that two programs written from scratch found (D-172). Historical *planned* M8 labels now map to the active hardening phase, including escape analysis (H25) and collector experiments (H26); they are not implemented promises. Valid TypeScript that the compiler can't lower yet is the compile error PTS1001 "… is not supported yet" (§2.3); the sections below name the main cases. Section 9 holds the implementation notes of each task.

---

## 1. Syntax and files

- Source files use the extension **`.pts`** (D-O01, D-004: the brief's original extension, confirmed by the owner after a brief switch to `.ps`). Declaration files stay `.d.ts`.
- `import "./x"` tries `x.pts` first, then the usual TypeScript extensions; `import "./x.pts"` names the file itself, and `./x.js` doesn't map to `x.pts` (D-001, D-004).
- Every file is an ES module in strict mode, whether or not it imports or exports anything (D-005). A `.ts` file reachable by imports is part of the program too (§6).
- The syntax is TypeScript plus one contextual keyword, `parallel` (§5).
- `parallel` is a keyword only in two positions; everywhere else it is an ordinary identifier, so existing code that uses it as a name keeps working:
  - `parallel <expression>` in expression position;
  - `parallel for (...)` in statement position.
- Disambiguation rules for `parallel` are in §5.3.

### 1.1 The compatibility principle

A `.pts` program that uses only plain TypeScript features (no `parallel`, no integer types, no `pts:` imports) must print exactly what Node prints when run as TypeScript, except where this spec documents a difference (§8).

- "Node" is Node 24 running the program as ES modules with its type transform (`--experimental-transform-types`, D-009). What must match is stdout and the exit code; stderr's text is not compared, but an uncaught error goes to stderr and exits with code 1, as in Node (D-O04).
- `pts:path`, `pts:process`, `pts:fs`, `pts:fs/promises` and `pts:assert` count as plain TypeScript: they stand for Node's `node:` modules of the same names, `node:assert/strict` for `pts:assert` (§7.3, D-171).
- The tests hold the implementation to this: every plain-TypeScript run test and docs sample, a corpus of 300 realistic programs (each that still diverges says why in a `// skip:` line that starts with its category: `by design`, `unsupported` or `bug`, D-149) and generated corpora of numbers and strings are compared with Node (`pts/tests/README.md`).

---

## 2. Toolchain and pipeline

### 2.1 Front end

- The front end is a fork of TypeScript 7 (typescript-go), pinned at `typescript/v7.0.2`. It provides the scanner, parser, binder, checker, and the language server that `pts lsp` serves (D-168).
- The compiler, `pts`, is written in Go inside the fork's module (it needs the fork's `internal/` packages). New code lives in `cmd/pts/` and `pts/`.
- Upstream modifications are minimal, marked `// PTS:` and listed in `docs/FORK.md`: the `.pts` extension in module resolution (D-001, D-004), the integer types as number types of their own in the checker (D-048), and `parallel` in the parser (D-120).
- Options are fixed (D-005): every program is checked with `strict`, target and module `ESNext`, `moduleResolution: bundler`, `allowImportingTsExtensions`, `moduleDetection: force`, `lib: ["esnext"]` (no DOM: `document` is an unknown name; `fetch`, `AbortController` and their classes are the prelude's, §7.1) and `types: []`. `tsconfig.json` is not read.
- The prelude, ParallelScript's globals (the numeric types, `console`, timers, `performance`, `process`, `fetch` and its classes, `AbortController` and its classes, `parallel`, `.par`), is a declaration file, `pts/lib/prelude.d.ts`, and a root file of every program. The `pts:` modules are ParallelScript sources, `pts/lib/*.pts` (D-116). Both are embedded in the compiler (served at `ptslib:///`), so the checker and the language server resolve them without files on disk.

### 2.2 Pipeline

1. TypeScript AST plus checker.
2. **PTS semantic checks**: the rules of this spec that tsc doesn't know (`pts/check`).
3. Lowering to a typed **ParallelScript IR**.
4. IR passes. There is no separate pass stage yet: whole-program analyses run before lowering (which variables a function other than their own assigns, whose narrowed reads are checked, `collectOutsideAssignments`, D-140) and at the end of it (the monomorphized instances that typed calls of erased copies look up, `specialize`, D-148; which captured locals live in cells, `markCells`; the checks of conversions to object types with optional properties, `checkRelaxedCasts`; the copy shapes that dynamic conversions need, `prepareDynamicTypes`), and others inside the C emitter (where evaluation order needs temporaries, which functions may recurse, generator frames, which descriptors carry declared types).
5. Emit **C11**.
6. Compile with **clang** (`-O2 -g -ffp-contract=off -fno-math-errno -falign-functions=32`: D-011, D-060, D-069, D-119) and link with the runtime into one native binary.

**The IR** is structured (not SSA) and fully typed. Boxing, closure environments, monomorphized generics, and async functions and generators (already turned into state machines) are all explicit in it. C is never emitted directly from the TS AST.

**Generated C** is readable and carries `#line` directives pointing at the `.pts` source, so gdb, lldb and crash backtraces show ParallelScript lines.

**The runtime** is written in C (`pts/runtime/`) and statically linked. Its dependencies are vendored as pinned submodules and built from source: bdwgc 8.2.12 (the collector), libuv 1.53.0 (event loops), PCRE2 10.49 (RegExp, D-132), Ryu 2.0 (number formatting, D-011) and libbacktrace (stack traces, D-069). A built program depends only on the C library (glibc's libc and libm); libgcc is linked statically.

`pts` finds its runtime sources through `PTS_HOME`, or else in the nearest directory above its own executable or the working directory that holds them. It builds each vendored library and the runtime once, into `~/.cache/pts/<name>-<key>/lib<name>.a`, where `<key>` hashes the compiler's version, the flags and the sources (`PTS_CACHE_DIR` replaces `~/.cache/pts`; a file lock makes concurrent builds share one), and compiles user programs against those cached static libraries (D-O11, D-003).

**Target:** x86-64 Linux with glibc. The stack guard (D-069) and the publication of new objects to other threads (D-130) rely on x86-64; other targets aren't supported yet. Cross-compiling with `pts build --cc zig` is a stretch goal.

### 2.3 The CLI

| Command | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `pts build file.pts [-o out]` | Build a native binary (default output: the file's base name, without `.pts`) |
| `pts run file.pts [args…]` | Build and run; arguments after the file go to the program |
| `pts emit-c file.pts [-o file.c]` | Print the generated C (or write it to a file), for debugging |
| `pts help [command]` | Show the usage, or a command's (as does `pts <command> --help`; D-170) |
| `pts version` | Print the version, the commit it was built from and typescript-go's version |
| `pts test [paths…]` | Build and run the test files under paths (default: the working directory): `name.test.pts`, `name-test.pts`, `name_test.pts`, `test-name.pts` and `test.pts`, searched recursively past `node_modules` and dot directories, and files named as paths. Each file runs on its own, one after another; the report is Node's spec reporter's, with the totals and failures of all files; exit code 1 if a test failed or a file didn't compile or ended before reporting (D-171, §7.3) |
| `pts lsp [--stdio]` | The language server, over stdin and stdout, for editors (D-168): typescript-go's own server, whose programs are the compiler's (its fixed options, the prelude, the `pts:` modules), with ParallelScript's checks (the numeric rules, the guardrail, the features left out) reported as diagnostics with `pts` as their source after tsc's, once a file and its imports have no tsc errors. Definitions in the library have `ptslib:///` URIs, whose text clients get through LSP 3.18's `workspace/textDocumentContent` (D-169). |

- **Exit codes** (D-006, D-179): 0 on success, 1 for compile or toolchain errors, 2 for a malformed command line, 3 for an internal compiler error (a bug in pts). `pts run` exits with the program's own code, or 128 + the signal number if the program was killed.
- **Diagnostics** (D-006): one per line, `file:line:col - error CODE: message`, the file relative to the working directory when it is below it. tsc's errors keep their `TSnnnn` codes; semantic errors are reported only when there are no syntax errors, as in tsc. A tsc error that ParallelScript's features lead to (a `.par` view's method called on an array, `.par` on a Map, `parallel (f)(x)`, `test.skip(…)`) is followed by an indented line with a hint that says what to write; the language server shows the hint as information at the error's range (D-170). ParallelScript's own codes:

| Code | Meaning |
|---|---|
| PTS1001 | Valid TypeScript the compiler can't lower yet ("… is not supported yet") |
| PTS1002 | A feature left out by design, with the reason (§3.10) |
| PTS2001 | A lossy implicit numeric conversion (§3.2) |
| PTS2002 | A literal that doesn't fit its numeric type, isn't an integer, or has a decimal point where an integer type is expected (D-170) |
| PTS2003 | Two integer types with no lossless common type |
| PTS3001 | The guardrail: an outer variable assigned in a parallel body (§5.6) |
| PTS3002 | What a `parallel for` can't hold (§5.5) |
| PTS9001 | An internal compiler error: a bug in pts, not in the program (a panic in the compiler, or C it generated that the C compiler rejects), at the source the compiler was working on (`file:line` without a column for the C compiler's errors), after the errors found before it, with a line asking for a report; `PTS_DEBUG=1` adds the compiler's stack or the C compiler's output (D-179) |

- **Environment variables:**

| Variable | Read by | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `PTS_HOME` | `pts` | The directory that holds `pts/runtime` |
| `PTS_CACHE_DIR` | `pts` | Where built libraries are cached (default `~/.cache/pts`) |
| `PTS_CC`, `PTS_AR` | `pts` | The C compiler and archiver (default `clang` and `ar`) |
| `PTS_DEBUG` | `pts` | `1`: an internal compiler error shows what a bug report needs, the compiler's Go stack or the C compiler's command line and output (D-179) |
| `PTS_THREADS` | programs | The number of event loops and pool threads (§5.1) |
| `GC_FREE_SPACE_DIVISOR`, `GC_INITIAL_HEAP_SIZE`, `GC_MARKERS` | programs | bdwgc's settings, which override the runtime's defaults (§4) |
| `TZ` | programs | The local time zone of `Date` (§7.2) |
| `PTS_TEST_RESULTS` | programs (`pts:test`) | Set by `pts test`: the file where `pts:test` writes a test file's totals and failures as JSON, instead of printing them (D-171) |

---

## 3. Types and values

### 3.1 Variables

- Variables have fixed types. `let x = 1; x = "s"` is a compile error (tsc already reports it).
- `let` and `const` are block-scoped; `var` is not supported (§3.10).
- Reading a module-scope binding before its declaration has run (from a function called early, or in an import cycle) throws `ReferenceError: Cannot access 'x' before initialization`, as in JS (D-021, D-022). So does a local variable that a closure reads or assigns before its declaration has run (a hoisted function called above a `const` it uses, D-140), and so do `new C()` and a static field of a class whose declaration hasn't run yet (D-141; for static methods and `instanceof` see §8).

### 3.2 Numbers

- `number` keeps JavaScript semantics exactly: IEEE-754 f64, including `NaN`, `Infinity` and `-0`.
- The added numeric types are `int` (meaning i64) and `i8 i16 i32 i64 u8 u16 u32 u64 f32 f64` (`f64` is `number`), declared in a global prelude.
  - They start as aliases of `number` in the prelude, so the editor accepts them immediately.
  - The numeric rules below are enforced by the PTS semantic pass, whose errors the language server shows too (D-168).
  - To the checker each is a number type of its own (D-048), so it can be an array element, a `Map` or `Set` key or value, a parameter or result of a function type, or a type argument (`u8[]`, `Map<string, int>`, `(n: int) => int`), and inferred types keep it: `ints.map(x => x * 2)` is an `int[]`. tsc relates every numeric type to every other, as it relates `number` to itself, so overloads match as they would for `number`: on a `u8[]`, `reduce(f, 0 as int)` takes `reduce`'s `T` overload (and fails the conversion rule); write `reduce<int>(f, 0)`.
- **Literals take their type from context.** `const x: u8 = 200` is fine; `const y = 5` is `number`, as in TS. So does an array literal's element or an argument (`bytes.push(7)` for `bytes: u8[]`); without a context a literal is a `number`, so `[byte, 1]` is a `number[]`.
  - An integer literal is checked exactly against its type's range, from its source text, so a 64-bit value keeps every digit (`const b: u8 = 256` is PTS2002, D-012).
  - A literal with a fraction next to an integer operand makes the operation a `number` (`n * 1.5`).
  - A literal written with a decimal point is never an integer: where its context makes it one, it is PTS2002 (`seven / 2.0` with `seven: int` would divide as integers; `const x: int = 2.0`), whose message says to write `2`, or to convert the other operand with `as number` (D-170). Exponents are fine: `const n: int = 1e6`.
  - Constant expressions are evaluated at run time, so they wrap rather than fail to compile: `const b: u8 = 200 + 56` is 0. A literal operand of `as` is a `number`, so `9007199254740993 as int` is 9007199254740992; annotate instead (`const x: int = 9007199254740993` is exact).
- **Implicit conversions are lossless only.**
  - Allowed implicitly: any integer type and `f32` to `number`; a smaller integer type to a larger one of the same signedness; an unsigned type to a strictly larger signed one (`u8` to `i16`). Not an integer type to `f32`, nor a signed type to an unsigned one (PTS2001).
  - Anything lossy needs `as`.
  - A function converts to a function type only if the type's parameters convert to its own and its result to the type's, losslessly: a `(b: u8) => void` can't be a `(n: number) => void`.
  - The operands of an operator convert to their common type: the wider of two compatible types, or `number` when an integer type meets `number` or `f32`. So mixing `int` and `number` in arithmetic yields `number`. Two integer types with no lossless common type (`i64` and `u64`) are PTS2003.
- **`as` between numeric types converts the value**, following Rust's rules: float to int truncates toward zero and saturates at the target type's range, and `NaN` becomes 0. Between integer types `as` wraps (modulo 2^n).
- **Integer arithmetic.**
  - Overflow wraps (two's complement), unary minus on an unsigned value included; `MIN / -1` is `MIN` and `MIN % -1` is 0.
  - Integer `/` is truncating integer division.
  - Integer `%` takes the sign of the dividend, as in JS and Rust (D-O17).
  - Integer division or modulo by zero throws `RangeError: Division by zero`; an integer `**` with a negative exponent throws `RangeError: Exponent must be non-negative` (D-012).
  - `f32` arithmetic is C's `float`; its `%` and `**` go through `double`.
- **Bitwise operators** (D-018): on `number` they have JS's int32 semantics exactly. On an integer type they work on the type's own bits: `&`, `|`, `^`, `~` as in C, `<<` wraps, `>>` is arithmetic for signed types and logical for unsigned ones, `>>>` shifts the bits as unsigned and keeps the type (`-16 as i32 >>> 0` is -16), and the shift count is taken modulo the type's width. The operands convert to their common type first, so `u8 << int` is an `int`, and an integer type meeting a `number` gives the int32 operation on numbers.
- `typeof` on any numeric type returns `"number"`.
- 64-bit integers keep every digit when printed, converted to strings (`toString(radix)` too) and in JSON (D-012, D-080, D-083). As a dynamic value (§3.4) an `int`, `i64` or `u64` is a number while its magnitude is at most 2^53 and a boxed integer beyond.
- Enum members (§3.13) and typed arrays' elements (§7.2) are `number`s: they convert to an integer type only with `as`.
- **Number-to-string conversion matches JS exactly**, using shortest round-trip formatting (Ryu, D-011) with the JS rules for choosing exponent or fixed notation. For example, `0.1 + 0.2` prints `0.30000000000000004`.

`docs/examples/spec_integers.pts`:

```ts
// SPEC §3.2: the integer types. Lossy conversions need `as`: `const b: u8 = half`
// would be PTS2001 ("a int doesn't convert to u8 implicitly").
const big: u8 = 200;
const sum = big + (100 as u8); // u8 arithmetic wraps
const half: int = 7 / 2; // integer division truncates
const mixed = half * 1.5; // an int with a fraction is a number
console.log(sum, half, mixed);
console.log(300.7 as u8, -1 as u8, NaN as int); // `as` truncates and saturates; NaN is 0
const bytes: u8[] = [1, 2, 3];
const doubled = bytes.map((b) => b * 2); // a u8[]
console.log(doubled, typeof doubled[0]);
const huge: int = 9007199254740993; // exact: every digit of an i64
console.log(huge + 1, (-16 as i32) >>> 0, (0xf0 as u8) >> 4);
try {
  console.log(half / (0 as int));
} catch (e) {
  console.log(String(e));
}

// expect:
// 44 3 4.5
// 255 0 0
// [ 2, 4, 6 ] number
// 9007199254740994 -16 15
// RangeError: Division by zero
```

### 3.3 Booleans and strings

- Strings are immutable, stored as UTF-8, and each one records whether it is pure ASCII and, where that is known, whether it is well-formed (holds no lone surrogate), so that RegExp matching can skip PCRE2's UTF-8 check (D-166).
- A lone surrogate, which JS strings may hold, is stored as its 3-byte (WTF-8) encoding, and concatenation joins two halves into one character, so string equality is byte equality; output writes a lone surrogate as U+FFFD, as Node does (D-014).
- Indexing keeps JavaScript's semantics so ported code behaves identically: `.length`, `s[i]`, `charCodeAt` and `slice` indices count UTF-16 code units.
  - ASCII strings take an O(1) fast path.
  - Non-ASCII strings are scanned from the start (O(n)) for now; a lazily built index is planned (D-016).
- `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=` compare strings by UTF-16 code units, as JS does.
- Building a string piece by piece (`s += x` in a loop) takes linear time: a long string's bytes go to an append buffer that later concatenations extend in place (D-061). So do loops over a global RegExp's matches (`replace`, `matchAll`, `split`, `exec` loops) and `split` by a string, on any string without lone surrogates (D-166).
- Case mapping, `localeCompare` and `normalize` use tables generated from Node's ICU (Unicode 17; D-016, D-082, D-135). Reading `s[i]` out of range follows §8.

### 3.4 `any` and `unknown`

`any` is a **checked dynamic value**.
- Anything is assignable to it.
- Inside the dynamic world, operations follow JavaScript semantics. Property access, calls, indexing and operators are resolved at runtime using type descriptors:
  - a missing property reads as `undefined`;
  - calling a non-function throws `TypeError`;
  - `+` concatenates when either side is a string.
- The check happens at the boundary into typed code. Assigning an `any` to a typed location verifies the value at runtime and throws `TypeError` on mismatch: `const n: number = parsed.count` throws if `count` isn't a number.
- This keeps `JSON.parse(s).user?.name` working while making `any` honest.
- What works (D-070 … D-079, D-089, D-091): reads, writes, `in` and destructuring of properties and elements; the operators (with JS's conversions: ToPrimitive, ToNumber, IsLessThan), `typeof`, equality; calls of functions and methods, whose arguments are checked as they enter typed code (missing ones are undefined, extra ones are ignored); the builtin methods of strings, numbers, booleans, arrays, maps, sets, dates, RegExps and functions; `Object.keys`, `values`, `entries` and `hasOwn`; `for…of`, spreads (into calls through `any` too, D-189) and array patterns, which iterate an array or tuple (live, by index), a string's code points, a set's elements or a map's entries (live), a generator or any object with a `[Symbol.iterator]()` method (one `next()` at a time for `for…of` and array patterns, closed when they stop early; H03, D-177), and throw `TypeError: x is not iterable` for anything else (D-078).
- Checked conversions reach every type: primitives, integer types, classes, interfaces and object types (with methods too), arrays, tuples, hash-map objects, maps, sets, function types and unions of them. A typed object whose declared types fit the target stays the same object; dynamic data (values of `any`, `any[]`, hash-map objects of `any`, maps and sets of other types), and typed objects whose declared types don't fit, are copied into the target's representation (§8). A union of object types takes a value as a member that declares every property of it that another member declares, as it is or by a copy, before a member that leaves one out, since narrowing by a tag or `in` may read it as any member that declares its properties (D-192). A function converts to a function type as it is if its signature is the type's, and otherwise through an adapter that checks its arguments and result when it is called (D-075). A write through `any` is checked against the place's declared type: `TypeError: Cannot set property p of C: expected T, got U` (D-072).
- Iterators through `any` (D-178): a generator's `next(v)`, `return(v)` and `throw(e)` (an async generator's give promises), the `next()` of the iterators of arrays, strings, maps and sets (which have no `return` or `throw`, as in JS), and an object iterator's own methods; maps', sets' and arrays' `keys()`, `values()` and `entries()`. A result the runtime makes is a plain object `{ value, done }`. As for every builtin method through `any`, these are found by calls only: `typeof h.next` is `"undefined"`.
- Not yet through `any`: typed arrays' methods that take callbacks (`TypeError`), and `.par` views.
- Every runtime type therefore carries a descriptor (name, field names mapped to offsets, methods). The same descriptors drive `console.log` and JSON.
- Representation (D-002): a dynamic value (an `any`, an `unknown`, a union) is one 64-bit word, encoded as JavaScriptCore encodes its values: an object is its raw pointer (so the collector sees it), a double is its bits plus 2^49 (`NaN` canonicalised), and `null`, `undefined`, `false` and `true` are small constants; a 64-bit integer beyond 2^53 is boxed. It isn't NaN-boxing with tagged pointers, and never a separate tag and payload, so it can't tear under races (§4.1).

`unknown` works exactly as in TypeScript: you must narrow before use. Narrowing an `unknown` to a class or a primitive (by `instanceof`, `typeof` or a type guard) and `as` from it are checked at runtime and throw `TypeError` when wrong (D-068). `x as any` and `x as unknown` box the value; `{}` written as a type, and the `object` type, are dynamic values too (D-089, D-091).

### 3.5 Casts

`as` on non-numeric types:
- Upcasts are free.
- Downcasts and casts to unrelated types are checked at runtime and throw `TypeError`; to an array or object type that isn't the value's own, `as` makes a checked copy, as converting from `any` does (D-089).
- `x!` is checked too, and throws `TypeError: Non-null assertion on null` (or `undefined`). Where there is no value to assert, `a.pop()!`, `a.shift()!`, `find(…)!`, `findLast(…)!` and `map.get(k)!` throw `RangeError` (D-038, D-042, D-046).
- `as unknown as T` cannot be used to lie about a value's type.
- `as const` and `as T` on an array or object literal give the literal that type, such as a tuple (D-053). `x satisfies T` is `x` (D-091).

### 3.6 Objects

- **Classes** have fixed layouts and use vtables; a method call is virtual only where a subclass overrides the method, otherwise direct (D-028). Supported (D-026, D-028, D-030, D-031): single inheritance (`extends`, `super(...)`, `super.m()`, also across modules), fields with initialisers, constructors, methods, getters and setters, parameter properties, static fields, methods, accessors and blocks, `private`, `protected` and `readonly` (checked by tsc only), `#private` fields and methods (`#x in o` for an `o` of an object type; of a dynamic value it is not supported yet), abstract classes, methods and fields, `instanceof`, `new.target`, `o.constructor.name`, generic classes (§3.7), and classes extending `Error` and its subclasses (§3.9). An override may leave out trailing parameters and return an instance of a subclass of what the overridden method returns (D-091).
  - Not yet (PTS1001): classes declared inside functions or blocks; class expressions and classes as values (`const K = Point`, a parameter of type `typeof Point`, `this` as a value in static code); extending builtins other than the error classes (`Map`, `Date`, `RegExp`, typed arrays); auto-accessors (`accessor x`); members with computed or quoted names, other than `[Symbol.iterator]` and `[Symbol.asyncIterator]`; index signatures in classes; an override that changes a parameter's type or leaves out a parameter with a default; a getter and a setter of different types; reading a property that has only a setter; assigning an inherited static field through a subclass; calling a static method that uses `this` through a subclass.
  - Decorators aren't supported yet (PTS1001).
- **Interfaces and object types stay structural**; tsc checks them. At runtime, a value used through an interface type dispatches through a table of field offsets and method pointers, one per (concrete type, interface) pair. Tables are built at compile time where possible and cached at runtime otherwise.
  - How (D-035, D-036): each descriptor lists its fields and its methods' entry points; each place that reads, writes or calls through an interface caches, in one word, the last descriptor it met and the field's offset or the method's index.
  - Interfaces and object types may declare methods and accessors, which class methods, getters and setters and object literals' members provide. A class or object provides a property only with the same representation: an `int` field doesn't provide a `number` property (PTS1001). A class's method may provide a method that takes more parameters (calls through the interface leave the rest out, through the method's adapter, D-149), but not leave out one the interface may omit that it requires.
  - Optional properties (`p?: T`, and mapped types: `Partial<T>`, `Required`, `Pick`, `Omit`, `Readonly`, `Record<"a" | "b", T>`) are fields of `T | undefined` that an object may leave absent: reading one gives undefined, `console.log` and `Object.keys` skip it and `"p" in o` is false (D-065). An object that lacks an optional property, or holds it without undefined, can still be used as the type (D-066, §8).
  - Not yet: optional methods and accessors, overloaded methods in object types, a property with a setter and no getter.
- **Object literals** become instances of anonymous struct types derived from their checked type ("shapes", shared by structurally identical types, with one descriptor per property order so that printing keeps the literal's order; D-033). Optional properties are stored as `T | undefined`. Literals may hold methods (closures that take the object as `this`), getters and setters (D-044, D-045). Spreads and rest properties are in §8 (D-058, D-108). Not yet: computed property names (other than in hash-map objects), methods or accessors beside a spread.
- **Properties cannot be added or deleted after construction.** `delete` only works on index-signature types (PTS1002 elsewhere). `Record<string, T>` and `{ [k: string]: T }` are hash maps under the hood:
  - *Hash-map objects* (D-059, D-093, D-097) are `Record<string, T>`, `Record<number, T>`, index-signature types (with required properties of the signature's value type too), object types whose property names are all array indices (`Record<Level, number>` for a numeric enum `Level`), and `Partial<Record<K, V>>`. They are ordered hash tables with string keys, listed in JS's order (array-index keys first, in numeric order, then the others in insertion order). Reading a missing key follows §8; `in`, `delete`, `Object.keys`/`values`/`entries`, `for…in`, JSON and `console.log` treat them as JS treats plain objects.
  - Not yet: index signatures with other key types (template patterns, symbols), index signatures mixed with methods, optional properties or properties of other types, recursive hash-map object types, methods in their literals, `toString` of one, and using an object of another type as one (its copy `{ ...o }` is one).
- **`null` and `undefined`** remain distinct values. Optional chaining (`?.` on properties, calls and elements, short-circuiting the rest of the chain) and `??`, `??=`, `||=` and `&&=` are supported (D-063, D-064).
- `Object`'s functions are in §7.2.

### 3.7 Unions and generics

- **Unions** are tagged values. Narrowing works with every form: `typeof`, `instanceof`, `in`, discriminant properties, equality and user-defined type guards. The checker does the reasoning; the backend emits tag tests.
  - How (D-062, D-067): a union is a dynamic value, one word whose encoding is its tag (§3.4, D-002), so it can't tear under races. A union of literals of one type is that type (`"a" | "b"` is a `string`); a union with an `any` or `unknown` member is dynamic.
  - Narrowing to a class is checked (`TypeError` if a type guard was wrong, §8), whether a union or a class's (or an interface's) value is narrowed to it (D-149). Members that a union's object types share are read and called through an interface made for the access; members of a union of objects and other values (`x.length` for `string | string[]`) are found at run time (D-089).
  - Not yet: unions of two numeric types (`u8 | i16`; literal unions are fine), writing a property that the members hold as different types, a union's method as a value.
- **Generics** are monomorphized.
  - A generic function has one instance per distinct list of type arguments, inferred or written, as their runtime types (D-049): `identity(1)` and `identity(1.5)` share `identity<number>`, while `identity(byte)` with `byte: u8` is `identity<u8>`, whose values keep their type. A function that computes types from its type parameters (a conditional type such as `Awaited<T>` or `T extends Id ? A : B`, an indexed access, `keyof`, a mapped or template literal type, in its signature or body) has one per distinct list of the types it computes for them, so `Promise<number>` and `Promise<string>`, `{ kind: "a" }` and `{ kind: "b" }`, or a branded `Id` and its `number`, which have the same runtime types, get their own instances where those differ (D-216).
  - Arithmetic on a value of a type parameter is done in `number`, as tsc types it, even when the instance's type argument is an integer type; `x as T` converts back by the numeric rules.
  - Generic interfaces and type aliases are instantiated like any other type (`Box<int>`).
  - A generic class has one class per instance (D-050), printed with the generic class's name (`Box { value: 1 }`); instances may extend classes and be extended (`class IntBox extends Box<int>`). Static members of a generic class are shared by all its instances, and `x instanceof Box` tests for any of them (D-089). Where tsc narrows x to the class's prototype's type, `Box<any>` (x's declared type isn't Box or derived from it: an interface Box implements, a base class, `object`), x is the instance its declared type implies, with the type arguments tsc infers from it through the class's `extends` and `implements` clauses and then structurally (a `Cache<string, number>` narrowed by `instanceof MapLRU` is a `MapLRU<string, number>`, an `Option<number>` narrowed by `instanceof Some` a `Some<number>`), and its members whose types in tsc mention `any`, and the objects held inside them (`c.matrix[0]`, `c.store.get(k)!`), are its own values held as dynamic values, so writes through them reach them and `===` compares them; a callback written as the argument of a method of such a value holds what the method hands it as it is, but a function given by reference or inside a literal argument with a parameter that would get a copy of the instance's objects (`c.items.forEach(pushOne)` for a `pushOne(x: any, i: number, arr: any[])` that writes `arr`) is a compile error, as is one such object that goes where a copy may be changed or kept (an `any[]` variable, a return); objects such a value puts into new containers (`[...c.matrix]`, `new Map(c.store)`) are copies there (H10b); where the declared type implies none (a plain base class, `object`, a union whose members imply different instances, a property declared `unknown`), x is a dynamic value whose members, private fields too, are found at run time (D-218). A value narrowed so that goes where tsc types `Box<any>` (`const y = x`, `[x]`, an inferred return type) throws a checked TypeError where it runs (H10b). Instances for type arguments of the same runtime types are one class, which the program may convert between as TypeScript allows (`Box<1>` to `Box<number>`). Only where a program doesn't compile that way is it compiled again with them split where the class computes, from its type parameters, a superclass or a member type that differs for them and that neither converts to the other or names other classes (`pick(v: R<T>): R<T>` with `R<T> = Box<T extends 1 ? string : number>` for `Holder<1>` and `Holder<number>`, D-216); converting between two such classes is a compile error.
  - Generic methods of classes, and generic functions nested in functions or methods, have instances too (D-051). A generic method may be abstract or override a generic method; calls of such methods go by name to erased copies (D-101).
  - Polymorphic recursion (`f<T>` calling `f<T[]>`) makes instances 64 deep along the chain of instances that make each other, up to 256 instances of each generic function declaration from each of the program's calls, and 1024 of each generic function or class declaration in all; past the depth or a call's 256 a direct call calls the erased copy (below), its result checked, so a program that recurses that deep or wide at run time runs on where its results are primitives and throws the checked TypeError where they hold generic class instances, and a call whose result would be copied (an array or another object type the erased copy would make) is a compile error, as are a generic class or a function value that would need instances past the limits and anything past the 1024 (D-198). Instances that a program's calls make side by side, outside any recursion, don't count against any limit.
  - Generic functions as values (D-078): a generic function declaration where tsc's context is a function type that isn't generic is the instance for the type arguments that context implies (`[1, 2].map(identity)`, `identity<number>` too); anywhere else (`const id = identity`), and every generic arrow function or function expression, it is an *erased copy*, one function whose type parameters take dynamic values: inside it, a value whose type mentions a type parameter is `any`, with `any`'s run-time checks, and a call of it converts its result to the type tsc gives the call with a check (`TypeError: Expected T, got U`). Arrays and objects pass to erased copies by reference. An erased copy that makes an instance of a generic class makes the instance for `any` (`Box<any>`), which doesn't convert to another instance (`Box<number>`). So a call of a generic function value, or of an abstract or overridden generic method, that knows its type arguments and whose result may hold such instances calls the monomorphized instance of its signature instead, which the value's function or the receiver's class lists (D-148): a generic function declaration, static method, or generic arrow function or function expression anywhere (D-190), whose instances run with the arrow's closure, so they see the variables it captures; a call whose result is a promise or generator of such instances finds one too. Calls through `?.`, by an element access whose key is a string literal or a const initialised with one (`m["make"](5)`), with rest parameters, and through interfaces, unions of object types and object literals' types (D-191) find their instances too (D-193). A generic method read as a value (also destructured), `bind` without bound arguments, and the adapter that an arrow property for an interface's generic method gets forward their typed calls to the instances of what they wrap; a generic value given where a function type that isn't generic goes (a typed declaration, an argument, a return, an array method's callback, `Array.from`'s mapper, a promise's callback, `f<number>`, a method that isn't generic) is adapted to the instance for the type arguments tsc infers there; an element-access call whose key's value only its type says (`m[key](5)`) finds the instance of the method the key names when it runs; and a dynamic argument that tsc types as a primitive (a `yield`'s value) passes to an instance found by name with a check (D-194). Values from `any` (unless they are the program's generic functions), calls inside erased code, calls by name passing an `any` value, and an instance that doesn't lower for its type arguments keep the erased copy (known issues). A program that its speculative instances would stop compiling is compiled with D-148's instances only (D-190).
  - A module-scope `const` holding a generic arrow function or function expression that makes instances of generic classes, or returns objects of a generic type (`List<T>`), is monomorphized where it is called by name, as a function declaration is (D-102, D-106).
  - Speculative ordinary function-value instances are omitted where immutable source flow proves that a call cannot reach them (D-201/D-202): const aliases, conditional/logical unions, comma RHS, unassigned parameters of closed non-generic named functions, and returns of proven synchronous non-generic functions. Return proofs join every possible return; nested callables' returns are not the factory's. Unknown sources, including values read back from `any` or uncertain containers, and async/generator/generic factories retain type-compatible instances. D-205 also tracks exact bound-method and zero-bound-argument bind creation sites, and captured expressions in value-forwarding wrappers and non-generic adapters. A site represents every closure/receiver made there, never an inferred receiver class. Unregistered or generic-context wrapper origins remain unknown. D-206 applies a separately cached receiver proof to direct named/interface, keyed/optional and dispatched method calls: actual directly named, unmodified non-generic class constructions can exclude impossible class and literal-method candidates. Inherited methods and override barriers remain available. Static receiver types are not origin proofs; generic contexts/constructors, uncertain constructor replacement (including ancestors), assertions, dynamic readback, mutable bindings and exhausted/cyclic proofs stay conservative. D-207 carries actual captured receiver expressions into method wrappers' inner demands, using an explicit bind's actual thisArg and joining every caller of a shared creation site. D-208 also follows selected data values in fresh object/array literals and const containers whose complete references are supported projection-only reads. It validates the entire literal and every binding use; a const/readonly annotation does not prove immutability. D-209 also follows simple immutable const container aliases, initialized directly through parentheses only. It resolves one literal root and validates every root/intermediate/leaf/sibling use before publishing any positive component fact. Every use must be a supported projection read or another eligible alias initializer; a mutation or escape anywhere invalidates the entire component. Each alias edge must preserve its checker data-member contracts (no function-as-method, optionality or array/tuple/union representation guess); const/readonly alone is still insufficient. Mutation, container escape, uncertain aliases, implicit method-call/tag `this`, dynamic/asserted origins, getters/setters/method members, computed/duplicate/spread/holey containers and unsupported keys retain compatible instances. Numeric keys require canonical nonnegative integer parser text (the parser can normalize source spellings); missing keys and exhausted/cyclic proofs are unknown, not empty. Source summaries remain separate for functions and receivers; only kind-independent structural container facts are shared. This is a bounded compile-size optimization, not a new restriction on values or a weaker checked-any boundary. D-210 extends static paths through directly nested inferred object/array data literals, such as `held.inner[0].factory`, with D-209 whole-root aliases. The complete nested tree and every component use must pass before any positive fact. D-211 also follows immutable const aliases initialized by static projection paths into those nested literals, including transitive aliases and repeated extractions of the same subtree. Each declaration denotes its selected subtree; every root, child and sibling use must pass before any positive component fact is published. Extraction and alias edges preserve declared checker data contracts, not flow-narrowed apparent types. D-212 also admits fresh inline literal roots, such as `const child = ({ child: { factory } }).child`. The complete raw literal tree and its one static path into the first const binding must pass before checking every subsequent alias use. Only that first edge normalizes fresh container identities along the finite literal syntax tree; downstream named edges keep raw declared-contract equality. Intermediate containers still cannot be stored, passed or returned. Contextual/generic nested trees and uncertain layouts remain unknown. Raw own/target contracts propagate along literal syntax edges only: equal required data keys, no method/accessor/optional/index/call/construct contracts, ordinary arrays and exact checker contracts at nonliteral callable/object terminals. Fresh container identities may differ only along those checked literal edges; this is not structural assignability. D-213 also proves immutable `const` object patterns that extract literal-valued child containers, including renamed/quoted keys and nested object patterns. Every identifier leaf must select a container; every sibling pattern, raw contract edge and transitive component use must pass before publication. The checker-inferred binding-pattern pseudo-context is not a declared conversion; actual annotations and contextual/generic origins remain conservative. D-214 also follows dense const array patterns over proven ordinary-array literal roots, including mixed nested object/array patterns, only when every identifier leaf selects a literal-valued child container. Array elements use the raw ordinary-array element contract; the entire pattern and component must pass before any positive fact. D-215 also permits present-index elisions in those finite ordinary-array patterns, including nested and all-elided subpatterns. Elisions preserve their original literal indices and consume bounded proof work without creating a binding or source; skipped terminal values are permitted only after the complete literal tree, raw contracts and every component use pass. Elisions or actual bindings past the finite literal end remain unknown. Direct fresh array patterns whose checker contract is a tuple remain unknown, as do defaults/rest, numeric/computed object keys, terminal-value leaves and explicit pattern-owner annotations. No tuple or heterogeneous-array layout barrier is relaxed. Runtime destructuring, captures, receiver identity and checked-any behavior are unchanged. Parameter/returned containers, remaining destructuring/nonliteral nested origins and broader caller-return flow remain H09b5c2b2b2b2b2b2b; no parent task or blocked H09b5b acceptance is implied.
  - A narrower fallback caps a speculative pairing or call site's availability by the code containing it (D-204): code present only at AllInstances cannot make an inner pairing survive at ValueInstances. Completed shared users and enclosing-class dependencies are considered; another live caller preserves the inner pairing. Unknown or budget-exhausted availability stays conservative. This changes neither pairing identity nor the fallback order, and does not weaken erased-copy or checked-any behavior.
  - Generic methods (D-079): an interface's or object literal's generic method has its erased signature and is called with its result checked; a class's generic method is monomorphized for calls through the class, and its erased copy serves calls through interfaces and `any`; a typed call through an interface, a union of object types or an object literal's own type whose result may hold generic class instances calls the instance of its signature that the object's class, or its literal's method closure, lists (D-191), as a dispatched call does (D-148), if each argument is a value of the instance's parameter type as it is (no copy, check, adapter or dynamic value) and no parameter typed with the type parameters is a dynamic value or holds, inside a union of object types, an instance of a generic class (whose erased copy's `Box<any>` never converts, D-192), and the erased copy otherwise; a generic method read as a value is the instance its context implies or the erased copy, bound to the object. An object that came through `any` with methods of other signatures than an interface's (an erased copy's `Box<any>` as a `Container<number>`) has them called through their dynamic entries, with checked results.
  - Not yet: generic functions declared inside generic functions or inside methods of generic classes, nested generic functions that use their outer function's variables, overriding a plain method with a generic one or a generic method with a plain one, generic accessors.

### 3.8 Equality

- `===` compares objects by reference identity, and strings and numbers by value, with JS's `NaN` rules.
- `==` is supported with its JS meaning for the types involved; in practice it is mostly `x == null`. With a union operand it is JS's IsLooselyEqual (D-067): null equals undefined only, booleans compare as numbers, strings convert as `Number(s)` does, and an object meets a primitive through its class's `valueOf()` if that returns a primitive, else its string (D-088).
- `Object.is` is SameValue; `Map` and `Set` keys and `includes` use SameValueZero.

### 3.9 Exceptions

- `throw`, `try`, `catch` and `finally` have JS semantics, and any value can be thrown. The catch binding is an `unknown` (D-068); `catch (e: any)` and `catch {}` work, while destructuring the catch binding is not supported yet.
- `Error` and its subclasses (`TypeError`, `RangeError`, `ReferenceError`, `SyntaxError`, `EvalError`, `URIError`, and `AggregateError` with `errors`, D-113) have `message`, `name`, `cause` and `stack`, and programs extend them (`this.name = …` or a `name` field, their own `toString`). `stack` is `Name: message`, then up to 10 frames `    at name (file.pts:line)` where the error was made (D-069): methods as `Class.method`, constructors as `new Class`, top-level code as `    at file.pts:line`, and code in a `pts:` module by the module's name (`pts:process:48`). A class derived from Error can't override `stack` yet.
- The errors of `pts:fs` are Node's system errors: an `Error` with `errno`, `code`, `syscall`, `path` and `dest`, typed `NodeJS.ErrnoException` (D-117). `fetch` rejects with Node's `TypeError: fetch failed` and a `cause`, and an aborted operation with a `DOMException`, an `Error` with a `name` such as `AbortError` and a legacy `code` (§7.1, D-160, D-162).
- Errors the runtime detects (integer division by zero, checked reads, failed `!` and casts, `ReferenceError` before a binding is ready, writes to frozen objects, members an asserted object literal lacks; D-157, D-158) are thrown as these classes and can be caught. Running out of memory can't be: it ends the program with `Uncaught RangeError: Out of memory …` and exit code 1.
- Unbounded recursion throws `RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded` (D-069), and so does a regular expression that exceeds PCRE2's backtracking limit (D-132).
- **Uncaught errors** (D-O07):
  - An uncaught exception prints `Uncaught ` and its stack (`<Name>: <message>` and its `.pts` frames) to stderr, then exits with code 1. Any other value prints as console.log shows it nested: `Uncaught 'text'`. An exception out of a timer, an immediate, a microtask or a task on another loop is uncaught the same way (D-114, D-121).
  - An unhandled rejection behaves like Node's default `--unhandled-rejections=throw`: once the microtasks have run, it ends the program as an uncaught exception does (D-112).
  - A top-level `await` that never settles prints Node's warning and exits with code 13, as Node does (D-113).

### 3.10 Not supported

Each of these gets a clear compile error explaining why (PTS1002, D-020; `with` is tsc's TS1101):
- `eval`, `new Function` (any use of the global `Function` as a value; the type `Function` is PTS1001, whose message suggests a function type, D-170), `with` (a strict-mode error, since every file is a module);
- `var` (the error suggests `let` or `const`);
- `arguments` (the error suggests a rest parameter);
- prototype manipulation: `__proto__`, `Object.setPrototypeOf`, assigning to `.prototype` or its members;
- `Proxy`, `Reflect`;
- declaration merging (function overloads, and interfaces that merge only with interfaces, are fine);
- namespaces (`namespace` and `module` declarations), until there's a reason for them; the error suggests ES modules;
- `bigint` types and literals: "BigInt is not supported yet" (D-O08); `BigInt64Array` and `BigUint64Array` too (PTS1001);
- `Symbol` and the `symbol` type, other than `Symbol.iterator` and `Symbol.asyncIterator` (D-O14);
- `delete` of anything but an entry of a hash-map object (§3.6);
- importing `pts:internal/…` outside ParallelScript's library (D-118).

Decorators and `WeakRef` can come later (D-L12): `WeakRef`, `WeakMap` and `WeakSet` are PTS1001 now, and so are decorators (§3.6).

### 3.11 Functions

- Function declarations (hoisted; nested ones to the top of their block), arrow functions, function expressions (a named one sees itself) and methods are values of function types (D-041): closures that capture variables by value, or in a shared cell when the variable may change after it is captured. A `for` loop's `let` variables are fresh per iteration; an arrow takes its method's or constructor's `this`, and `super.m()` works in it.
- Parameters may have defaults (evaluated in the callee's scope when the argument is left out or `undefined`), be optional or be rest parameters (of array or tuple types), and be destructuring patterns (D-054, D-055, D-057, D-063, D-091).
- `f.call`, `f.apply` and `f.bind` (with leading arguments), `f.name` and `f.length` (D-044, D-045, D-098). A method read as a value is bound to its object (§8).
- A function value converts to another function type when its parameters and result do (losslessly for numbers, §3.2); one that takes fewer parameters, or returns a value where the type returns nothing, goes through an adapter, except an arrow function or function expression written in place, which takes the context's shape (D-041).
- Function overloads (declarations and class methods): the implementation runs, and its result converts to the chosen overload's result type with a check (D-117).
- `console.log` shows functions as Node does: `[Function: f]`, `[Function (anonymous)]`, `[Function: bound f]`, `[GeneratorFunction: g]`, `[AsyncFunction: f]`, `[AsyncGeneratorFunction: f]`.
- Not yet: `this` parameters; overloaded and recursive function types; converting a function to a string; spreading an array into parameters other than a rest parameter (a tuple's elements may be spread into any), and spreading a set or a string into arguments; spreads in `call`, `apply` and `bind`; a callback that returns nothing passed to `map`, `flatMap` or `reduce`; the `thisArg` argument of array methods; a method of a string, an array or a map read as a value without a call (`s.trim`, `xs.push`), though Math's functions, `String`, `Number`, `Boolean` and the `Number.is…` tests are values (D-083, D-084, D-089).

### 3.12 Arrays and tuples

- `T[]` holds its elements unboxed in their own representation (numbers as doubles, a `u8[]` as bytes, objects as pointers; D-038), and arrays are race-safe (§4.1). An array can't be given holes, but it can hold them: `new Array(n)` leaves holes in arrays of strings, objects and dynamic values (D-085), a match array has one for each group that took no part (D-133), and racing pushes can leave one (D-130). A hole reads as undefined where any value can go; reading out of range follows §8. The array methods treat holes as JS's do (D-183): the callback methods skip them (`map`'s result keeps them), own keys and `in` leave them out, `concat` keeps them, `pop` and `shift` give undefined for one, and the reads JS makes with Get (iteration, spreads, `Array.from`, `find` and its kin, `includes`, the copying methods) give undefined for one where the element type holds undefined; a match's group that took no part stays an undefined element wherever its array's elements go.
- An array converts to an array type of other elements (`Dog[]` as `Animal[]`) by a copy, each element converted with a check, only where the copy can't be told apart from the array: a new array, a `const` whose only use is the conversion, or an argument that the callee only reads. Elsewhere it is PTS1001 ("… where it may be changed or kept", D-094). A shorthand property (`{ a }`), a shorthand's default (`{ a = ns } = o`) and `export { a }` are uses, and an exported `const` is never the only use (D-181).
- Tuples of fixed length are objects with typed, unboxed elements, printed as arrays (D-053); optional elements (`[string, number?]`) work, an array or a tuple shorter by optional elements converts to the tuple type, leaving them out (D-181), and a tuple type with a rest element (`[number, ...number[]]`) is an array (D-100). An index into a tuple must be a constant; a tuple used with the array methods or `for…of` is copied into an array (D-089).
- Destructuring (with defaults, rest elements and nesting) and spreads take arrays, tuples, strings, maps, sets and iterables (D-054, D-058, D-110). A spread is read where it is, as JS iterates it: a later argument or element that changes the spread array, set or iterable doesn't change what it spreads (D-189).
- `flat(depth)` and `flatMap` flatten as JS does, whatever the elements' static types (D-180): every element that is an array or a tuple at run time is spread (an element of a union, `any`, `unknown` or `object` type, a tuple, a tuple receiver), as many levels as the depth says (any expression, converted as JS converts it: ToNumber, then ToIntegerOrInfinity; 1 when left out or `undefined`), holes are skipped at every level (a match's groups that took no part are undefined elements, and kept), and the result has tsc's type for the call. An element stored in the result is checked against its element type where its own type doesn't say it is one, so a result never holds what its type doesn't (`TypeError: Expected Point[], got Point`). `flatMap` visits the elements the array has when it starts, skipping holes. A depth that tsc types as a negative or fractional number literal (`xs.flat(-1)`, `xs.flat(1.5)`, or a union with one) is PTS1001, as is a `flatMap` callback returning an array or tuple whose elements aren't of its result's element type (`xs.flatMap<number[]>((x) => [x])`): tsc types those results as JS doesn't make them; the depth's message gives the depth JS uses. Elements a `flatMap` callback's values hold convert to the result's element type as an array literal's would, and one whose copy JS wouldn't make is PTS1001 (D-181): a class instance of another layout than an interface (as `const js: J[] = [c]` is), an array of other elements that isn't new at each call (a `const` of the enclosing code is the same array at each call), a hash-map object, map or set of other types, and an object literal the callback doesn't make as the result's interface (annotate the mapper or the callback); dynamic data is copied (D-071).
- Typed arrays and `ArrayBuffer` are in §7.2.
- Not yet: recursive array types (`type Nested = Nested[]`), `flat` with a negative or fractional literal depth (above), `copyWithin`, `for…of` without a declaration of its variable.

### 3.13 Enums

- Numeric, string and `const` enums compile to their members' constants (`E.m`, `E["m"]`, also across modules); an enum type is the number or string type of its members, so a numeric member converts to an integer type only with `as` (D-052).
- `E[n]` of a numeric enum is the reverse mapping, `E[E.m]` included (D-099); a value no member has follows §8.
- An enum object used as a value (`console.log(E)`, `Object.keys(E)`) is a hash-map object of its members and of the reverse mappings (D-089), a new one at each use (§8).
- Not yet: members whose values aren't constants (`A = s.length`).

### 3.14 Generators and iteration

- Generator functions (`function*` declarations, expressions, and methods of classes and object literals) with `yield` and `yield*`, and generator objects with `next`, `return` and `throw`, where `return()` runs `finally` blocks (D-109). They compile to state machines over collected frames (§5.2).
- The iteration protocol (D-110, D-111, D-146): `for…of`, spread, `Array.from`, `new Set`/`new Map`, array patterns and `yield*` take arrays, strings, maps, sets, typed arrays, generator objects and any object whose `[Symbol.iterator]()` method gives an iterator (a generator object, or an object with `next()` and, optionally, `return()` and `throw()`), read a result's `done` before its `value`, and call `return()` when a loop ends early; a loop that ends by a throw keeps its error if closing the iterator throws too.
- `for…of` and array patterns step an iterator one `next()` at a time whether the iterable is typed or `any`, so an infinite one works (D-177). An array pattern binds each element (its default, its nested pattern) before it steps for the next, takes `undefined` once the iterator is done, gives a rest element all that is left, and closes the iterator at its end unless it is done, or when binding an element throws. A name a declaration's own pattern binds past the end of what it destructures (anything but a tuple: an array, string, set, map, typed array, iterator, iterable or `any` value) is `undefined`, as for arrays (D-172); in parameter patterns, `for…of` bindings, nested patterns and patterns inside a rest element, a typed name still throws there (RangeError for an array, a checked TypeError for an iterable) unless the value is `any` (H11). Spread, `Array.from`, `new Set`/`new Map` and rest elements take everything, as in JS. Maps, sets and typed arrays iterate live through `any` and `Iterable<T>` too.
- A getter typed `void` (its body only throws or returns nothing) or `never` reads as `undefined` (D-177). An iterator result is the object the iterator handed back (D-178): `it.next()` on a typed iterator gives the object an object's own `next()` returned, unread, so its getters run when and as the caller reads them; a `yield*` hands its inner iterator's result objects through to its caller (GeneratorYield(innerResult)); an elision in an array pattern reads only `done`, and closing an iterator reads nothing of what `return()` gave. An object literal with getters or more properties, or a class's instance, can be a typed result (`next(): IteratorResult<T>`, `x as IteratorResult<T>`): `done` and `value` are read by name off an object of another layout than the runtime's own results, converted to their types with a check; passed where an interface goes, such an object is converted to it as dynamic data is (§2, D-071: a copy if its declared types don't fit, without its identity), and a `value` holding dynamic data that its type copies (parsed JSON where an interface goes) is copied at each read. A `yield*` reads its last result's `value` once done, used or not. A hand-written result without `done` reads it as `false` through a result type of one shape (`Iterator<T, T>`), where JS gives `undefined`; destructuring a result (`const { value } = it.next()`) and calling a function-typed `value` directly (`r.value()`; read it into a local first) are PTS1001. `Iterable<T>`, `Iterator<T>` and `IterableIterator<T>` values are dynamic values. The iterators of maps, sets, arrays and strings are values, live as JS's are (`m.keys().next().value`).
- A `yield` may be anywhere in an expression; the operands JS evaluates before it are kept in the generator's frame with the values they had then, a compound assignment whose right side yields reads its target first, a call finds the function it calls before its arguments, and `??=`, `||=` and `&&=` yield only when they store (D-111, D-145, D-187, §8).
- Async generators and `for await` are in §5.2.
- Not yet: a `yield` (or `await`) in a loop's condition or update, a case label, a declaration's variables after the first, a default value or an optional chain; `yield*` in async generators; iterator helpers (`it.map(…)`); a static or generic `[Symbol.iterator]`, and reading `x[Symbol.iterator]` without calling it.

---

## 4. Memory and the shared heap

- Memory is garbage collected by **bdwgc** (Boehm–Demers–Weiser) in thread-aware mode with thread-local allocation and parallel marking.
- Objects are allocated with precise layouts: each object's descriptor holds bdwgc's mark descriptor, so numbers in fields are never taken for pointers (D-024; an object of more than 62 words is scanned whole). Pointer-free data, such as string bytes and numeric arrays, is allocated with the atomic (no-scan) allocator. Stacks are scanned conservatively.
- The runtime's defaults (D-060, D-112, D-131): the collector runs once the program has allocated as much as the heap holds (free-space divisor 1, as Go's GOGC=100 does), the heap starts at 4 MiB, and marking uses 2 threads. bdwgc's `GC_FREE_SPACE_DIVISOR`, `GC_INITIAL_HEAP_SIZE` and `GC_MARKERS` override them.
- A precise collector, and later a generational one, is planned (M8).

### 4.1 The race-safety invariant (non-negotiable)

A program with data races may observe stale or surprising values, but it must never crash the runtime, corrupt the heap, or read or write out of bounds (Java's guarantee, not Go's). Concretely:

- **No torn values.** Any value in a heap slot that can be read concurrently is one machine word (a pointer, a small scalar or a box), or is written and read atomically. No multi-word values that can tear in shared fields. The C-level rules that give this (which accesses are atomic, which are plain one-word ones and what that assumes of clang and x86-64, reading a slot once before checking it, no `memcpy` of shared numbers, state one thread owns) are the shared-memory contract in `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` §4.13 (D-143).
  - Typed arrays' elements are read and written by relaxed atomic accesses of their width, and the methods that copy or move them in bulk (`set`, `slice`, `copyWithin`, `reverse`, `sort`, construction from another typed array, `ArrayBuffer`'s `slice`) copy by relaxed atomic units of the element's size or wider, so racing threads may see a stale number in an element, never a torn one or bytes outside the view (D-125, D-143). Views of one buffer with different element types see each other's bytes; an element is whole only to views of its own width or narrower.
- **No out-of-bounds writes.** Growable arrays keep their capacity inside the backing buffer, and bounds checks use the length and capacity of the buffer actually being accessed. Racing `push`es can lose elements but never write past the end.
- **Strings are immutable**, so sharing them is free.
- What races look like (D-130): a racing operation may throw `RangeError` where a sequential one couldn't (a `pop` of an array another thread emptied, a write past a length another thread shrank); a push lost to another can leave a hole, which reads as undefined where any value can go and throws `RangeError` where an element is required; a map, set or hash-map object that threads change at once may lose entries, hold a key twice and report a size that differs from its entries (never below 0). Share tables through `pts:sync`'s `Mutex`. `pts/tests/stress/race_*` test the invariant.
- **Generators and iterators shared by threads** (D-143): one thread at a time runs a generator's body; `next()`, `return()` or `throw()` while another thread runs it throws `TypeError: Generator is already running`, as a resume from its own body does. Each resume's value goes to the thread that asked for it, though threads racing on a generator or a runtime iterator (over an array, a tuple, a map or a set) may skip or repeat items. An async generator runs on the loop that made it: requests from other loops queue there in order, so each item goes to exactly one request.
- **Promises shared by loops** (D-121, D-143): a handler that another loop adds counts from the moment it is added, so a rejection that its home loop settles meanwhile isn't reported as unhandled.
- **Signals and event targets shared by threads** (D-185): each `EventTarget` (so each `AbortSignal`) has its own lock over its listeners and abort state, so threads may add and remove listeners, follow a signal with `AbortSignal.any`, give it to `fetch` and abort it at once: every listener and follower is kept, a signal aborts once, and a `once` listener runs once. A listener runs on the thread that aborts the signal or dispatches the event. A dispatch under way on one thread may still call a listener another thread removes meanwhile.
- New objects are published to other threads by plain stores, which x86-64 never reorders with the stores that filled them; a port to another architecture needs release stores there (D-130).

---

## 5. Concurrency

### 5.1 The model

- The runtime starts one event loop per core. The count is configurable with `PTS_THREADS`.
- Each loop runs on its own OS thread, and all loops share one heap.
- Each loop is a libuv `uv_loop_t` with its own timers, immediates and microtask queue; `uv_async_send` wakes a loop from another thread. `pts:fs/promises` runs its system calls on libuv's threadpool and settles their promises on the loop (D-118). Sockets come with `pts:http`'s server (D-136): its connections are libuv TCP handles on the loop that accepted them (the loop that called `serve`, or with `reusePort` any loop, D-137), and a loop flushes stdout before it waits for I/O. `fetch`'s connections are libuv TCP handles on the loop that called it (for a pool task, its operation's loop), its host names resolved with libuv's `getaddrinfo` (D-160, D-162). There is no API of its own for sockets, DNS or child processes yet.
- How (D-121): the main thread's loop runs the program's modules; the other loops start the first time `parallel` runs, so a program that never uses it has one thread. `PTS_THREADS` is a positive integer (values above 256 count as 256); without it, or with anything else, the count is the number of cores the process may run on.
- Timers and immediates belong to the loop that made them (D-139). Any thread, another loop's or a pool thread running a `.par` callback, may pass one to `clearTimeout`, `clearInterval` or `clearImmediate`, or call its `ref`, `unref`, `hasRef` or `refresh`: clearing takes effect at once (a callback that hasn't started never starts), `hasRef` sees the new state at once, and the timer's loop applies the rest (its heap, whether it keeps the loop alive, `refresh`'s new due time) when it next takes its messages.
- A timer is due its delay after it is set or refreshed, as Node's are. Unreferenced timers (`unref()`, `AbortSignal.timeout`'s) fire at their time on every loop while the program runs, as Node's do on its one loop: a loop with nothing referenced left sleeps until its first timer is due, and then runs it if some loop still has work. They never keep the program running (D-186).
- The program ends when every loop has nothing left to do at once: nothing referenced to wait for (timers, I/O, as in Node) and no task or message waiting for it. A task nobody awaits keeps the program running until it is done.
- `process.exit`, an uncaught exception and an unhandled rejection on any loop end the whole program at once.

### 5.2 Plain `await`

Plain `await` keeps exact JavaScript semantics.
- A task belongs to one loop for its whole life, and a loop runs one task at a time.
- Code between two `await`s therefore runs without interference from other tasks on the same loop.
- For single-loop code, the ordering of microtasks, timers and I/O callbacks matches Node.

Async functions and generators compile to state machines. Locals that live across an `await` or `yield` are stored in a GC-managed frame object; there is no separate native stack per task.

- What there is (D-112 … D-115): async functions (declarations, expressions, arrows, and methods of classes and object literals); `Promise` with ECMAScript's algorithms in V8's job order, thenables of the program's included (`then` read once, a throw from reading it rejecting, D-144); async generators, and `for await` over async generators, objects with `[Symbol.asyncIterator]()` and sync iterables of promises; Node's timers and immediates and `queueMicrotask` (§7.1).
- Top-level `await` (and `for await`) works in the entry module; in other modules it is not supported yet (D-113).
- An `await` may be wherever a `yield` may (§3.14). Not yet: an `await` in a loop's condition or update (`while (await next())`), a case label, a declaration's variables after the first, a default value or an optional chain. An expression-bodied async arrow may await anywhere in its body (`async () => (await p) + 1`, D-172).

### 5.3 Task parallelism: `parallel <expr>`

- `parallel e` evaluates `e` as a new task on another loop and immediately returns a `Promise<Awaited<T>>`, where `T` is the type of `e` (the promise is flattened if `e` is itself a promise).
- Captured variables are shared by reference, because the heap is shared.
- `await parallel f(x)` is the common idiom (`docs/examples/spec_tasks.pts`):
  ```ts
  // SPEC §5.3: `parallel e` runs e as a task on another event loop and gives a promise of
  // its value at once.
  // threads: 1 2 4
  function fib(n: number): number {
    return n < 2 ? n : fib(n - 1) + fib(n - 2);
  }
  async function report(name: string): Promise<string> {
    return `${name}: ${fib(20)}`;
  }

  console.log(await parallel report("alice"));
  const results = await Promise.all([20, 21, 22].map((n) => parallel fib(n)));
  console.log(results);
  try {
    await parallel JSON.parse("{");
  } catch (e) {
    console.log("rejected with", (e as Error).name);
  }

  // expect:
  // alice: 6765
  // [ 6765, 10946, 17711 ]
  // rejected with SyntaxError
  ```
- The target loop is picked by load (least-loaded, or round-robin with stealing of tasks that haven't started). A task never migrates once it has started.
- Promises can be awaited from any loop; settling one wakes the loop that is awaiting it.
- With `PTS_THREADS=1`, `parallel e` still creates a new task, on the same loop (D-O06).
- How (D-121):
  - A task goes to the least-loaded loop other than the one starting it, where a loop's load is the tasks placed on it and not finished (ties go round-robin). A loop that runs out of work takes a task another loop has queued but not started before it sleeps.
  - A promise belongs to the loop that made it and settles there; its reactions (an `await`, a `then` handler) run on the loop that added them. So `parallel e`'s promise settles on the loop that ran `parallel`, after e's value (or the outcome of the promise e gives) is known, and an unhandled rejection of it is reported there, as for any promise.
  - A throw in e, synchronous or not, rejects the task's promise.
  - An `await` or `yield` in the operand (outside functions in it) is not supported yet; await before `parallel`, or give it an async function's call.
  - Known gap: a loop busy with something referenced (a long timer, a server) doesn't take tasks queued on others until it runs dry. (A handler added from another loop and an async generator driven from several loops are safe, §4.1, D-143.)

**Parsing `parallel`** (D-O05):
- `parallel` is the operator only when the next token is on the same line and starts an expression **other than** `(`, `[`, `.`, `?.`, a template, `=`, an operator, `,`, `;`, `)` or `}`. Examples: `parallel foo(x)`, `parallel new X()`, `parallel async () => …`, `parallel await x`, `parallel "lit"`.
- `parallel for (` is the loop statement (§5.5).
- `parallel(expr)` parses as a normal call. The checker reinterprets it as the operator **only if** no binding named `parallel` is in scope and the call has exactly one argument.
- How (D-120): `parallel e` is a call of the prelude's `parallel<T>(task: T): Promise<Awaited<T>>` marked by the parser, so tsc types both forms; the operand is a unary expression, as `await`'s (`parallel f(x) + 1` is `(parallel f(x)) + 1`), or an arrow function. `parallel` followed by `=>`, `:`, `?`, `!`, `++`, `--`, `as`, `satisfies` or `of` is also the identifier. `parallel e` where a binding named `parallel` is in scope is a compile error (PTS1002). Any operand is the operator's, a function or class expression too, whose task makes the value (D-150, ruling 1 of `docs/agent/OWNER_ANSWERS.md`).

### 5.4 Data parallelism

**The pool.** A work-stealing fork-join pool with Chase–Lev deques, one per worker, sized to the core count and separate from the event-loop threads. A thread that starts a parallel operation helps execute work until the operation finishes. The design follows Rayon (`join`, adaptive splitting).
- How (D-122): `PTS_THREADS` − 1 workers (the count of §5.1), started at the first parallel operation, since the thread that starts one works too; a loop thread gets its own deque then. `join(a, b)` offers b to thieves, runs a, then runs b itself unless it was stolen, in which case it runs other work until b is done; a throw out of either is rethrown once both are done, a's first. Operations over index ranges split with Rayon's adaptive splitter (as many pieces as threads when nothing is stolen; a stolen piece splits again), down to a minimum piece length each operation chooses.
- A callback that a pool thread runs for an operation of loop L works for L: the promises it makes belong to L, and their reactions, its `then` handlers and its `queueMicrotask` jobs run on L once L is free again (after the operation). Timers and asynchronous I/O (`pts:fs/promises`, `serve`) started from such a callback on a pool worker throw `Error` for now; `fetch` works there, its request running on L (D-162). On a loop's thread that helps L's operation, a timer or server starts on that thread's own loop, which then clears or stops it when asked from any thread (D-186).

**The `.par` view.** `.par` on arrays, typed arrays and ranges returns a parallel view with: `map`, `filter`, `forEach`, `reduce(fn, initial)`, `flatMap`, `some`, `every`, `find` (any match), `count`, `sum`, `min`, `max`, `sort`/`sortBy` (parallel merge sort) and `toArray`.
- These methods are synchronous: they return when the work is done.
- `.par.map` preserves order.
- `reduce`'s function must be associative.
- How (D-123): `xs.par` is a `ParallelArray<T>` of `pts:parallel` (a library written in ParallelScript); `range(start, end).par` a `ParallelRange`. `filter` keeps order; `find` gives any match (the leftmost among the pieces that ran) or undefined; `some`, `every` and `find` stop early; `reduce(fn, initial)` folds the elements with `fn(a, b)`: each piece from its own first element, then the pieces' results, with `initial` in front once, so it equals `reduce` for any initial value (`initial` for no elements; D-151); `count(predicate)` counts the elements it holds for; `sum`, `min` and `max` take the elements as numbers, as `Number` and `Math.min`/`Math.max` do (0, Infinity and -Infinity for none); `toArray()` copies. Holes are as Array's methods have them (D-183): `map` keeps them, the other methods skip them (`find` too, which gives what Array's gives), and `count`, `sum`, `min` and `max` take only the elements there are. `sort(compare?)` is Array's sort (in place, stable, the elements' strings without a comparator; undefined after the others and holes last, which the comparator never sees; on several threads a copy is sorted and written back, so a comparator that throws leaves the array as it was, D-182) and `sortBy(key)` sorts in place by keys (numbers or strings) computed in parallel, both as a parallel merge sort (D-124); `flatMap` flattens one level, in order, as Array's does: arrays and tuples the callback returns are spread and holes skipped (D-180). A `flatMap` callback declared to return a tuple, returning a tuple or an array it didn't make whose type isn't the result's (`pairs.par.flatMap((p) => p)` for a `[string, number][]`), or a named function whose result type has such an array, is PTS1001, since it would be copied into the result's array type (D-094); `(p) => [...p]` works. One returning an array whose elements aren't of the result's element type throws a TypeError, as the result's checks do. Promise work in callbacks run on other threads (a `then`, `queueMicrotask`) is queued on the calling loop before the method returns.
- A `ParallelArray`'s callbacks take (element, index, array), as Array's do; a `ParallelRange`'s take the integer only, and its `sort(compare)` and `sortBy(key)` make new arrays.
